[4:32.64] digital landscape. In 2008, the term

[4:52.72] to software development and IT
[4:54.72] operations. How is DevOps different from
[4:57.84] traditional models? DevOps differs from
[5:01.12] traditional models of software
[5:02.72] development and IT operations in several
[5:05.28] key ways. Traditional models often

[5:16.40] and continuous delivery. Here are some

[5:21.44] models and traditional models.
[5:24.32] Development and operations teams often
[5:26.80] work in separate departments leading to
[5:29.20] communication barriers and delays in the
[5:31.76] software delivery process. DevOps

[5:36.24] communication between the operations
[5:38.16] team, development team, and other
[5:40.00] stakeholders. Teams work together
[5:42.64] throughout the entire development life
[5:44.72] cycle, fostering a culture of shared
[5:47.52] responsibility.
[5:49.36] Traditional models rely heavily on

[5:54.56] deployment, testing, and infrastructure
[5:57.20] provisioning. This manual approach is
[5:59.60] time-conuming and is also prone to
[6:01.84] errors. DevOps advocates for automation

[6:07.04] cycle. Automation tools and scripts are

[6:22.16] Changes are not frequently released

[6:33.44] enables organizations to meet customer

[264:16.56] so since docker is a containerization
[264:19.04] platform It's important to understand
[264:21.60] what came before containerization or
[264:23.60] what is the history of containerization.
[264:25.76] So before docker came into the picture
[264:27.60] before containerization came into the
[264:29.60] picture there was this concept of
[264:31.12] virtualization or basically using
[264:33.76] virtual machines. So virtualization was
[264:36.24] this technique of importing a guest OS
[264:39.04] on top of an operating system. And this
[264:41.76] technique was a revelation at the
[264:44.08] beginning because it allowed developers
[264:46.00] to run multiple operating systems in
[264:48.32] different virtual machines all running
[264:50.40] on the same host operating system which
[264:52.80] nothing but eliminated the need for
[264:55.12] extra hardware resources. Now the
[264:57.68] advantages of virtual machines or of
[265:00.16] virtualization are many. Multiple oss
[265:03.36] could be run on the same machine. The
[265:05.60] maintenance and recovery was easy in
[265:07.76] case of failure conditions. And the
[265:09.60] third point here being the total cost of
[265:12.00] ownership was also less due to the
[265:14.16] reduced need for infrastructure. So as
[265:16.56] you can see here on your screen right
[265:18.32] now, you can see that there is a host
[265:20.40] operating system on which there are
[265:22.32] multiple guest oss running which is
[265:24.88] nothing but a virtual machine. So as
[265:27.44] most concepts have their shortcomings,
[265:29.92] virtualization also had a few. So
[265:33.12] running multiple virtual machines on the
[265:35.84] same host OS each to the performance of
[265:38.72] degradation. This is because the guest
[265:41.44] OS running on top of the host OS will
[265:44.00] have its own kernel own set of libraries
[265:46.48] and own dependencies and these take up a
[265:49.52] large chunk of the system resources that
[265:52.00] is the hard disk, processor, RAM and
[265:54.08] other resources. Another problem with a
[265:56.56] virtual machine which used
[265:57.92] virtualization that it takes a lot of
[265:59.92] time to boot up. So the problem is very
[266:02.64] critical in case of realtime
[266:04.80] applications. So these drawbacks were
[266:07.60] always there. Apart from that, you also
[266:10.40] have the age-old battle between
[266:12.64] development and production teams that
[266:15.12] the code works at development and does
[266:17.76] not work in productions because the
[266:20.00] developer has a system with their own
[266:22.56] set of libraries, their own kernel and
[266:24.80] the apps running on there whereas the
[266:27.44] production team has these resources of
[266:29.92] their own. So this is a problem that you
[266:32.08] can blame upon the difference in the
[266:33.60] computing environment. A code that runs
[266:35.92] on the developer system might not run on
[266:38.64] someone else's computer. So this led to
[266:40.96] a new technique called containerization.
[266:43.92] Basically a container brings
[266:45.44] virtualization to the OS level while
[266:48.48] virtualization brings abstraction to the
[266:51.04] hardware. Containerization brings
[266:53.20] abstraction to the OS. So what you might
[266:55.60] notice here is containerization is
[266:57.76] basically virtualization but it's more
[267:00.80] efficient because there is no guest OS
[267:02.88] here. It utilizes a host's operating
[267:06.00] system, shares relevant public libraries
[267:08.72] and resources where needed and unlike
[267:11.20] virtual machines, the application
[267:13.20] specific libraries and binaries of
[267:15.92] containers run on the host kernel. So
[267:19.20] each app has its own set of libraries
[267:21.44] and binaries in its own little container
[267:24.16] which makes processing and execution
[267:26.24] extremely fast. Even if you have to
[267:28.88] boost a process, it takes only a
[267:30.72] fraction of a second cuz in case of
[267:33.12] containerization, all the containers
[267:35.36] share only the host operating system but
[267:38.32] hold all their application related
[267:40.24] binaries and libraries in themselves.
[267:42.88] They are lightweight and faster than
[267:44.80] virtual machines. So here as you can see
[267:48.00] there is your host OS or your host
[267:50.32] kernel which is shared by all the
[267:52.48] different containers. So the containers
[267:54.96] themselves only contain the application
[267:57.84] specific libraries which are separate
[267:59.76] for each container. This is what makes
[268:01.92] them faster and they do not waste any
[268:04.16] resource. All these containers are
[268:06.72] handled by a containerization layer
[268:09.76] which is not native to the host OS.
[268:12.24] Hence a software is needed which can
[268:15.28] enable you to run containers on your
[268:17.92] host OS. And this is also how
[268:20.16] containerization solves the difference
[268:22.32] in computing environment problem. Now a
[268:25.36] developer works on containers instead of
[268:28.48] virtual machines. The app and its
[268:30.64] required libraries and binaries all are
[268:33.04] in one container. So when it is passed
[268:35.52] on to the testing team or the production
[268:37.84] team, it does not matter whether their
[268:40.40] host systems have the same libraries.
[268:43.52] All the dependencies are already present
[268:46.00] in the container containing the app. So
[268:48.88] now that you know what is virtualization
[268:51.52] and what is containerization and why do
[268:54.64] we need containerization let's move
[268:56.88] ahead and talk about docker. Why do we
[269:00.24] need docker? So a challenge I briefly
[269:03.28] spoke about earlier is what I would like
[269:05.36] to elaborate on in this section. Now
[269:08.16] when you have a project code in a
[269:10.08] development life cycle there are
[269:11.76] different different environments. You
[269:13.68] have your virtual server, you have your
[269:15.60] staging environment, you have your
[269:16.80] production environment, you have your QA
[269:18.56] environment. Now, most of the code that
[269:21.04] we deploy today is done using VMs or
[269:23.60] virtual machines. Now, how this works is
[269:26.08] that a developer or certain developers
[269:28.24] will write the code and all of it is
[269:30.40] placed in a version control system such
[269:32.24] as Git. Now, this could also be placed
[269:34.48] in a staging server depending on your
[269:37.20] organization's infrastructure, but most
[269:39.76] organizations these days use a version
[269:41.68] control system. So more often than not
[269:44.08] the code is placed in that version
[269:45.68] control system. Now this code is never
[269:48.24] directly deployed in the production
[269:50.32] server. It's usually first deployed in a
[269:53.12] lower life cycle server like your QA
[269:55.44] server or your staging server. Now once
[269:58.32] it is successful in that then only it is
[270:01.28] deployed on your production server. Now
[270:03.76] this is where the chances of conflicts
[270:06.32] increase. So the same piece of code that
[270:08.64] might be running on your staging server
[270:10.56] might not run on your production server.
[270:12.80] And the reason is very simple is the
[270:14.72] difference in the computational
[270:16.08] environment. This is also known as
[270:18.64] infrastructure incompatibility. Now in
[270:21.12] most cases your QA servers or your QA
[270:23.84] environment, staging environment are
[270:25.68] always updated. In most organizations
[270:28.00] that's the case. It usually has the
[270:30.00] latest libraries, latest binaries,
[270:32.16] latest jars, all that jazz. But the same
[270:34.88] cannot be said for the production
[270:36.40] environment. So in order to face all
[270:38.96] these challenges that is faced defense
[270:41.92] environment and lack of optimization of
[270:44.40] resources, we use docker. So this is
[270:46.96] where docker comes into picture. Docker
[270:49.44] does virtualization in the software
[270:52.00] level and we call it containerization as
[270:54.40] I had mentioned before. So here as you
[270:56.80] can see containers are bundled with
[270:58.56] their own set of libraries and binaries
[271:01.04] but they can communicate with each other
[271:02.88] with a fixed set of protocols. The thing

[380:25.31] [Music]
[380:30.24] Let's start with understanding why
[380:32.16] should you use terapform. There are some
[380:34.72] great pre-existing tools like anible
[380:37.36] chef puppet and salt stack. But then
[380:40.24] what makes terraform better than these
[380:42.24] tools? First reason is that terraform is
[380:45.68] a server orchestration tool. All the
[380:48.16] above mentioned tools were created for
[380:50.40] server configuration meaning that their
[380:52.72] primary goal is to install and manage
[380:55.12] software on the already existing server.
[380:58.00] Terraform concentrates more on server
[381:00.40] provisioning with software container
[381:02.56] deployment left to docker and packer.
[381:05.76] The next reason is the immutable
[381:07.76] infrastructure
[381:09.36] with chef's salt puppet or anible any
[381:12.48] software update must be run in place.
[381:15.12] Thus, every server grows a unique record
[381:17.84] of updates throughout its life cycle.
[381:20.24] This can quite often lead to so-called
[381:22.16] configuration drift when the differences
[381:24.32] in these configurations lead to bugs.
[381:27.28] Terraform addresses the issues by
[381:29.84] utilizing an immutable infrastructure
[381:32.08] approach where every new update of any
[381:34.88] parameter creates a separate
[381:36.64] configuration snapshot. This way,
[381:39.12] updating the development environment
[381:41.20] goes smoothly and is completely
[381:43.04] bugproof. The next reason is the
[381:45.84] declarative code style. While Chef or
[381:48.72] Ansible forces you to write step-by-step
[381:51.12] procedural instructions for reaching the
[381:53.60] desired state, Terapform prefer
[381:55.76] describing the desired each state of the
[381:58.32] system and the tool itself deals with
[382:01.12] reaching the goal state. But why is it
[382:03.92] better? With the procedural code, you
[382:06.40] need to think of all the recent events
[382:08.48] and processes that took place in order
[382:10.88] to write clear instructions. But with
[382:13.44] Terapform you simply order the tool to
[382:15.92] do something with the currently active
[382:17.92] state of the system. This is why the
[382:20.16] code base remains quite small and easily
[382:22.80] understandable.
[382:24.40] The fourth reason is the client only
[382:26.64] architecture.
[382:28.24] Terraform leverages the cloud providers
[382:30.80] API for provisioning the infrastructure
[382:33.52] which removes the need of additional
[382:35.60] security checks running a separate
[382:37.84] configuration management server and
[382:40.00] multiple software agents. Ansible does
[382:43.20] this by connecting through SSH yet the
[382:46.16] capabilities are quite limited.
[382:49.20] So what is Terraform? Terraform is an
[382:52.64] open-source infrastructure as a code
[382:54.72] tool developed by Hashior Corp. It is
[382:57.52] used to define and provision the
[382:59.44] complete infrastructure using an easy
[383:01.60] tolearn declarative language called HCL
[383:04.88] or Hashi Cororp configuration language.
[383:08.00] Terraform supports multiple providers
[383:10.40] such as AWS, Azure, Kubernetes, Google
[383:14.08] Cloud and many more. Now let's
[383:17.12] understand Terraform better through a
[383:19.04] use case. Rapid is an agile innovative
[383:22.64] company. They are providers of strategic
[383:25.20] cloud consulting, cloud migration,
[383:27.36] DevOps automation, managed services and
[383:30.48] security consulting. So once their
[383:33.12] client was looking for a cloud agonistic
[383:35.68] tool to orchestrate the infrastructure
[383:38.08] onto multiple public clouds they wanted
[383:40.88] the infrastructure to be up in a matter
[383:43.04] of few minutes. The client had different
[383:45.76] environments like production staging and
[383:48.72] QA to test. Database backups were also
[383:52.16] included as a part of infrastructure
[383:54.48] setup. DevOps engineers at Rapidar
[383:58.16] reviewed and analyzed the client's
[384:00.00] requirements and came up with a solution
[384:02.48] of using Terraform to codify the
[384:05.04] provisioning of infrastructure onto
[384:07.36] public cloud. Not only infrastructure
[384:09.84] but rapid also automated the application
[384:12.48] deployment process by integrating GitLab
[384:15.52] to improve the software development and
[384:17.84] accelerate the software delivery. The
[384:20.48] solution provided by Rapidar also took
[384:22.80] care of database backup for a situation
[384:25.44] when the infrastructure is destroyed.
[384:27.92] Using this solution, the client is now
[384:30.40] able to test their application and
[384:32.56] deploy it on production environment
[384:34.80] without downtime.
[384:36.96] So the overall benefits that a client
[384:39.44] could experience include the client's
[384:41.60] ability to create the infrastructure
[384:43.60] over multiple public clouds within a
[384:45.92] matter of few minutes. Next is that the
[384:48.64] client has the flexibility to launch the
[384:50.72] infrastructure as and when needed.
[384:53.44] Furthermore, the automated deployments
[384:55.92] help the client push the application
[384:58.08] into production seamlessly, thereby
[385:00.48] reducing the time to market. Also, the
[385:03.52] automated backup of the database ensures
[385:06.24] that they always have a copy of their
[385:08.32] latest database.
[385:10.72] Moving ahead, let's understand Terraform
[385:13.20] life cycle. It includes initialize,
[385:16.72] plan, apply and destroy. Terapform in it
[385:20.08] initializes the working directory which
[385:22.72] consists of all the configuration files.
[385:25.52] Terapform plan is used to create an
[385:28.00] execution plan to reach a desired state
[385:30.40] of the infrastructure. Terraform apply
[385:33.44] then makes the changes in the
[385:35.04] infrastructure as defined in the plan
[385:37.76] and the infrastructure comes to the
[385:39.76] desired state. Terapform destroy is used
[385:42.72] to delete all the old infrastructure
[385:44.88] resources which are marked tainted after
[385:47.28] the apply phase.
[385:49.68] Now let's see how terapform works.
[385:53.04] Teraphform core uses two input sources
[385:55.60] to do its job. The first input source is
[385:58.88] a terapform configuration that you as a
[386:01.52] user configure. Here you define what
[386:04.00] needs to be created or provisioned. The
[386:06.72] second input source is a state where
[386:08.96] terapform keeps the upto-date state of
[386:11.36] how current setup of infrastructure
[386:13.76] looks like. So what terapform code does
[386:17.04] is it takes the input and it figures out
[386:19.68] a plan of what needs to be done. It
[386:22.40] compares the state and checks what is
[386:24.80] the current state and what is the
[386:26.56] configuration that you desire in the end
[386:29.04] result. It figures out what needs to be
[386:31.76] done to get that desired state in the
[386:34.00] configuration file. The second component
[386:36.80] of architecture is providers for
[386:39.52] specific technologies.
[386:41.76] This could be cloud providers like AWS,
[386:44.32] Azure or other infrastructure as a
[386:46.80] service platform. It gives you the
[386:49.12] possibility to create infrastructure on
[386:51.28] different levels. Terraform has over
[386:54.16] hundreds of providers for different
[386:56.00] technologies and each provider then
[386:58.64] gives Teraphform user access to its
[387:00.96] resources.
[387:02.48] For example, AWS provider gives you
[387:05.36] access to hundreds of AWS resources like
[387:08.48] EC2 instances, the AWS users, etc. With
[387:12.96] Kubernetes provider, you can access
[387:15.36] commodities, resources like services and
[387:18.48] deployments, namespace, etc. So this is
[387:22.16] how Terraform works and this way it
[387:24.88] tries to help you provision and cover
[387:27.12] the complete application setup from
[387:29.60] infrastructure all the way to the
[387:31.60] application.
